I know that probably sounds like a lot for just seeing a balloon, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction to balloons happen when you are alone with a balloon, outside of a school setting?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I think I notice it much more at school. Like, the second I spot balloons there, even from down the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing. \n\nIf I were alone with one somewhere else… I imagine I’d still feel uneasy, but I guess the school setting is where it really shows up for me. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same reaction to balloons if you were to see them in a different school, or is it something about your current school specifically that contributes to your reaction?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it would happen in another school too, if I saw balloons there. I don’t know if it’s only my current school, exactly. What brings me in is that at school, the second I spot balloons—even across a hallway—my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing.  \n\nSo I guess it’s more the school setting with balloons than just that one building, if that makes sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… uncomfortable, but I try not to make a huge thing of it. I’d say it’s enough that I change what I’m doing, like asking my aides to handle birthday circles if there are balloons, or I’ll move away from festival tables where they’re set up.

Completely stopped… I don’t think I’d put it that strongly. I’m still doing my normal day-to-day things, mostly.

A little, yes… mostly at work. I don’t think my friends have really been affected much, but with colleagues it’s been awkward because I’ve had to hand off normal classroom celebrations to my aides when balloons are involved.

My supervisor noticed, which made me feel pretty embarrassed. I hope I’m explaining it okay — it’s not that I’m trying to avoid people, exactly, it just ends up looking that way sometimes.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess what brings me in is something that feels a little silly to say out loud, but at school, if I see balloons—even just across the hallway—my chest tightens right away and I kind of freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing for a few seconds. I know that probably sounds small, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things besides balloons that cause this kind of reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Not really other things, no… it’s mostly balloons specifically. I mean, I know they’re just decorations, especially in a classroom, and I feel a bit embarrassed saying this, but when one is stretched really tight or bobbing near the children, I immediately feel like it could pop right in my face. And I get scared in a way that feels… bigger than it should, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has anything unpleasant or traumatic ever happened to you involving balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wouldn’t call it traumatic exactly, maybe that sounds too dramatic, but about seven months ago a balloon burst really close to my face during a children’s art celebration. Since then, this has kind of been happening. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction happen if you see a balloon in a different context, like at home or in a picture?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mainly at school that I notice it, especially if I spot balloons even from across the hallway. It’s almost immediate—my chest tightens and I sort of freeze for a moment before I can get back to what I’m supposed to be doing. I know that probably sounds like a lot for just seeing a balloon, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction to balloons happen when you are alone with a balloon, outside of a school setting?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I think I notice it much more at school. Like, the second I spot balloons there, even from down the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing. \n\nIf I were alone with one somewhere else… I imagine I’d still feel uneasy, but I guess the school setting is where it really shows up for me. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same reaction to balloons if you were to see them in a different school, or is it something about your current school specifically that contributes to your reaction?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it would happen in another school too, if I saw balloons there. I don’t know if it’s only my current school, exactly. What brings me in is that at school, the second I spot balloons—even across a hallway—my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing.  \n\nSo I guess it’s more the school setting with balloons than just that one building, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding going to places or events where you think balloons might be present?\n\nPatient: A little bit, yeah… I mean, I still try to do what I’m supposed to do, but lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circles if there are balloons involved. And if there are festival tables or decorations with balloons nearby, I kind of move away from that area.\n\nIf I absolutely have to stay, I just keep some distance and focus on getting through it without looking at them too much. I hope that makes sense—I’m not trying to avoid my responsibilities, exactly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you have to avoid places or events because of balloons?\n\nPatient: It’s… uncomfortable, but I try not to make a huge thing of it. I’d say it’s enough that I change what I’m doing, like asking my aides to handle birthday circles if there are balloons, or I’ll move away from festival tables where they’re set up.\n\nIf I have to stay, I can usually manage by keeping some distance and just not looking at them much. But I do feel embarrassed about it, and kind of tense the whole time. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a social or work obligation because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes… a few times, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. At work, it’s started to get in the way a little. I’ve had to ask my aides to take over normal classroom celebrations when balloons were there, and I missed helping with a festival art table that I had actually planned because there were balloons nearby.\n\nMy supervisor noticed that one, which was embarrassing. I still try to do my job, but… yes, I guess I have avoided some work things because of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine that you have completely stopped doing because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… I don’t think I’d put it that strongly. I’m still doing my normal day-to-day things, mostly.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been adjusting around it—like asking my aides to handle birthday circles if there are balloons, or moving away from festival tables when balloons are nearby. And if I really have to stay, I keep some distance and just try to get through it without looking at them too much.\n\nSo… not completely stopped, I guess, but I am changing how I handle certain parts of the day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of balloons is interfering with your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… mostly at work. I don’t think my friends have really been affected much, but with colleagues it’s been awkward because I’ve had to hand off normal classroom celebrations to my aides when balloons are involved.\n\nAnd there was that festival art table I was supposed to help with, the one I had planned, and I didn’t end up doing it because there were balloons nearby. My supervisor noticed, which made me feel pretty embarrassed. I hope I’m explaining it okay — it’s not that I’m trying to avoid people, exactly, it just ends up looking that way sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, I know they’re just decorations, especially in a classroom, and I feel a bit embarrassed saying this, but when one is stretched really tight or bobbing near the children, I immediately feel like it could pop right in my face. Since then, this has kind of been happening. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction happen if you see a balloon in a different context, like at home or in a picture?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mainly at school that I notice it, especially if I spot balloons even from across the hallway. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same reaction to balloons if you were to see them in a different school, or is it something about your current school specifically that contributes to your reaction?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it would happen in another school too, if I saw balloons there. I don’t know if it’s only my current school, exactly. What brings me in is that at school, the second I spot balloons—even across a hallway—my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing.  \n\nSo I guess it’s more the school setting with balloons than just that one building, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding going to places or events where you think balloons might be present?\n\nPatient: A little bit, yeah… I mean, I still try to do what I’m supposed to do, but lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circles if there are balloons involved. I don’t think I’m doing a terrible job or anything, but it has started to affect work in some situations.\n\nLike, when there are balloons for classroom celebrations, I’ve had to hand that off to my aides instead of handling it myself. Um… besides work, I’m not sure it’s causing big problems, honestly. I mean, I’ll avoid them if I notice them, or I might choose to stand somewhere else at an event, but I haven’t really stopped doing important things outside of work because of it.

I know that probably sounds like a lot for just seeing a balloon, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction to balloons happen when you are alone with a balloon, outside of a school setting?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I think I notice it much more at school. Like, the second I spot balloons there, even from down the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing. \n\nIf I were alone with one somewhere else… I imagine I’d still feel uneasy, but I guess the school setting is where it really shows up for me. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same reaction to balloons if you were to see them in a different school, or is it something about your current school specifically that contributes to your reaction?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it would happen in another school too, if I saw balloons there. I don’t know if it’s only my current school, exactly.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say a significant part of my life, exactly… at least I don’t think so. But lately I have been avoiding certain parts of things.

Like at school, if there are balloons for birthday circles, I’ll ask my aides to handle that part. And at events, if there’s a table or area with balloons nearby, I’ll kind of move away or not stay there. If I absolutely have to be near them, I try to keep some distance and just focus on getting through it without looking at them too much.

I know that probably counts as avoiding, but I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess what brings me in is something that feels a little silly to say out loud, but at school, if I see balloons—even just across the hallway—my chest tightens right away and I kind of freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing for a few seconds. I know that probably sounds small, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things besides balloons that cause this kind of reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Not really other things, no… it’s mostly balloons specifically. I mean, I know they’re just decorations, especially in a classroom, and I feel a bit embarrassed saying this, but when one is stretched really tight or bobbing near the children, I immediately feel like it could pop right in my face. And I get scared in a way that feels… bigger than it should, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has anything unpleasant or traumatic ever happened to you involving balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wouldn’t call it traumatic exactly, maybe that sounds too dramatic, but about seven months ago a balloon burst really close to my face during a children’s art celebration. Since then, this has kind of been happening. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction happen if you see a balloon in a different context, like at home or in a picture?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mainly at school that I notice it, especially if I spot balloons even from across the hallway. It’s almost immediate—my chest tightens and I sort of freeze for a moment before I can get back to what I’m supposed to be doing. I know that probably sounds like a lot for just seeing a balloon, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction to balloons happen when you are alone with a balloon, outside of a school setting?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I think I notice it much more at school. Like, the second I spot balloons there, even from down the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing. \n\nIf I were alone with one somewhere else… I imagine I’d still feel uneasy, but I guess the school setting is where it really shows up for me. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same reaction to balloons if you were to see them in a different school, or is it something about your current school specifically that contributes to your reaction?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it would happen in another school too, if I saw balloons there. I don’t know if it’s only my current school, exactly. What brings me in is that at school, the second I spot balloons—even across a hallway—my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing.  \n\nSo I guess it’s more the school setting with balloons than just that one building, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding going to places or events where you think balloons might be present?\n\nPatient: A little bit, yeah… I mean, I still try to do what I’m supposed to do, but lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circles if there are balloons involved. And if there are festival tables or decorations with balloons nearby, I kind of move away from that area.\n\nIf I absolutely have to stay, I just keep some distance and focus on getting through it without looking at them too much. I hope that makes sense—I’m not trying to avoid my responsibilities, exactly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you have to avoid places or events because of balloons?\n\nPatient: It’s… uncomfortable, but I try not to make a huge thing of it. I’d say it’s enough that I change what I’m doing, like asking my aides to handle birthday circles if there are balloons, or I’ll move away from festival tables where they’re set up.\n\nIf I have to stay, I can usually manage by keeping some distance and just not looking at them much. But I do feel embarrassed about it, and kind of tense the whole time. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a social or work obligation because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes… a few times, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. At work, it’s started to get in the way a little. I’ve had to ask my aides to take over normal classroom celebrations when balloons were there, and I missed helping with a festival art table that I had actually planned because there were balloons nearby.\n\nMy supervisor noticed that one, which was embarrassing. I still try to do my job, but… yes, I guess I have avoided some work things because of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine that you have completely stopped doing because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… I don’t think I’d put it that strongly. I’m still doing my normal day-to-day things, mostly.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been adjusting around it—like asking my aides to handle birthday circles if there are balloons, or moving away from festival tables when balloons are nearby. And if I really have to stay, I keep some distance and just try to get through it without looking at them too much.\n\nSo… not completely stopped, I guess, but I am changing how I handle certain parts of the day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of balloons is interfering with your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… mostly at work. I don’t think my friends have really been affected much, but with colleagues it’s been awkward because I’ve had to hand off normal classroom celebrations to my aides when balloons are involved.\n\nAnd there was that festival art table I was supposed to help with, the one I had planned, and I didn’t end up doing it because there were balloons nearby. My supervisor noticed, which made me feel pretty embarrassed. I hope I’m explaining it okay — it’s not that I’m trying to avoid people, exactly, it just ends up looking that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of balloons is affecting your overall job performance?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t think I’m doing a terrible job or anything, but it has started to affect work in some situations.\n\nLike, when there are balloons for classroom celebrations, I’ve had to hand that off to my aides instead of handling it myself. And I missed helping with a festival art table that I had planned because there were balloons nearby, and my supervisor noticed that.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is affecting my job performance somewhat, even if I’m trying to manage it and not let it show too much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work, where you feel like your fear of balloons is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Um… besides work, I’m not sure it’s causing big problems, honestly. I mean, I’ll avoid them if I notice them, or I might choose to stand somewhere else at an event, but I haven’t really stopped doing important things outside of work because of it.\n\nI guess it’s more inconvenient than life-limiting outside the classroom, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're avoiding a significant part of your life because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say a significant part of my life, exactly… at least I don’t think so. And at events, if there’s a table or area with balloons nearby, I’ll kind of move away or not stay there. If I absolutely have to be near them, I try to keep some distance and just focus on getting through it without looking at them too much.\n\nI know that probably counts as avoiding, but I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of balloons is something that you think about or worry about even when you're not actually seeing balloons?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I worry about it constantly or anything like that. It’s not like balloons are on my mind all day.\n\nBut if I know there’s going to be a school event or a birthday thing, I might wonder ahead of time if there will be balloons there. I try not to dwell on it, though. Yes… a little, I guess. If I’m going somewhere and I think there might be balloons, I do get kind of tense beforehand.

It’s not that I think something terrible is definitely going to happen, but when I picture one of those tight balloons, or one bobbing around near kids, I start thinking it could pop right near me. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess what brings me in is something that feels a little silly to say out loud, but at school, if I see balloons—even just across the hallway—my chest tightens right away and I kind of freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing for a few seconds. I know that probably sounds small, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things besides balloons that cause this kind of reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Not really other things, no… it’s mostly balloons specifically. I mean, I know they’re just decorations, especially in a classroom, and I feel a bit embarrassed saying this, but when one is stretched really tight or bobbing near the children, I immediately feel like it could pop right in my face. And I get scared in a way that feels… bigger than it should, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has anything unpleasant or traumatic ever happened to you involving balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wouldn’t call it traumatic exactly, maybe that sounds too dramatic, but about seven months ago a balloon burst really close to my face during a children’s art celebration. Since then, this has kind of been happening. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction happen if you see a balloon in a different context, like at home or in a picture?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mainly at school that I notice it, especially if I spot balloons even from across the hallway. It’s almost immediate—my chest tightens and I sort of freeze for a moment before I can get back to what I’m supposed to be doing. I know that probably sounds like a lot for just seeing a balloon, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction to balloons happen when you are alone with a balloon, outside of a school setting?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I think I notice it much more at school. Like, the second I spot balloons there, even from down the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing. \n\nIf I were alone with one somewhere else… I imagine I’d still feel uneasy, but I guess the school setting is where it really shows up for me. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same reaction to balloons if you were to see them in a different school, or is it something about your current school specifically that contributes to your reaction?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it would happen in another school too, if I saw balloons there. I don’t know if it’s only my current school, exactly. What brings me in is that at school, the second I spot balloons—even across a hallway—my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing.  \n\nSo I guess it’s more the school setting with balloons than just that one building, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding going to places or events where you think balloons might be present?\n\nPatient: A little bit, yeah… I mean, I still try to do what I’m supposed to do, but lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circles if there are balloons involved. And if there are festival tables or decorations with balloons nearby, I kind of move away from that area.\n\nIf I absolutely have to stay, I just keep some distance and focus on getting through it without looking at them too much. I hope that makes sense—I’m not trying to avoid my responsibilities, exactly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you have to avoid places or events because of balloons?\n\nPatient: It’s… uncomfortable, but I try not to make a huge thing of it. I’d say it’s enough that I change what I’m doing, like asking my aides to handle birthday circles if there are balloons, or I’ll move away from festival tables where they’re set up.\n\nIf I have to stay, I can usually manage by keeping some distance and just not looking at them much. But I do feel embarrassed about it, and kind of tense the whole time. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a social or work obligation because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes… a few times, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. At work, it’s started to get in the way a little. I’ve had to ask my aides to take over normal classroom celebrations when balloons were there, and I missed helping with a festival art table that I had actually planned because there were balloons nearby.\n\nMy supervisor noticed that one, which was embarrassing. I still try to do my job, but… yes, I guess I have avoided some work things because of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine that you have completely stopped doing because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… I don’t think I’d put it that strongly. I’m still doing my normal day-to-day things, mostly.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been adjusting around it—like asking my aides to handle birthday circles if there are balloons, or moving away from festival tables when balloons are nearby. And if I really have to stay, I keep some distance and just try to get through it without looking at them too much.\n\nSo… not completely stopped, I guess, but I am changing how I handle certain parts of the day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of balloons is interfering with your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… mostly at work. I don’t think my friends have really been affected much, but with colleagues it’s been awkward because I’ve had to hand off normal classroom celebrations to my aides when balloons are involved.\n\nAnd there was that festival art table I was supposed to help with, the one I had planned, and I didn’t end up doing it because there were balloons nearby. My supervisor noticed, which made me feel pretty embarrassed. I hope I’m explaining it okay — it’s not that I’m trying to avoid people, exactly, it just ends up looking that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of balloons is affecting your overall job performance?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t think I’m doing a terrible job or anything, but it has started to affect work in some situations.\n\nLike, when there are balloons for classroom celebrations, I’ve had to hand that off to my aides instead of handling it myself. And I missed helping with a festival art table that I had planned because there were balloons nearby, and my supervisor noticed that.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is affecting my job performance somewhat, even if I’m trying to manage it and not let it show too much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work, where you feel like your fear of balloons is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Um… besides work, I’m not sure it’s causing big problems, honestly. I mean, I’ll avoid them if I notice them, or I might choose to stand somewhere else at an event, but I haven’t really stopped doing important things outside of work because of it.\n\nI guess it’s more inconvenient than life-limiting outside the classroom, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're avoiding a significant part of your life because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say a significant part of my life, exactly… at least I don’t think so. And at events, if there’s a table or area with balloons nearby, I’ll kind of move away or not stay there. If I absolutely have to be near them, I try to keep some distance and just focus on getting through it without looking at them too much.\n\nI know that probably counts as avoiding, but I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of balloons is something that you think about or worry about even when you're not actually seeing balloons?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I worry about it constantly or anything like that. It’s not like balloons are on my mind all day.\n\nBut if I know there’s going to be a school event or a birthday thing, I might wonder ahead of time if there will be balloons there. I try not to dwell on it, though. I’m not sure if that counts as worrying, exactly… but it does come up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious before going to a place where you think balloons might be present?\n\nPatient: Yes… a little, I guess. If I’m going somewhere and I think there might be balloons, I do get kind of tense beforehand.\n\nIt’s not that I think something terrible is definitely going to happen, but when I picture one of those tight balloons, or one bobbing around near kids, I start thinking it could pop right near me. I know it’s probably just decoration and not a big deal, but in the moment it feels bigger than it should. I mean, there are normal things I don’t love, like anyone, but I don’t think I have that kind of reaction to other objects or situations. It’s mostly just the balloons. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

It’s almost immediate—my chest tightens and I sort of freeze for a moment before I can get back to what I’m supposed to be doing. I know that probably sounds like a lot for just seeing a balloon, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction to balloons happen when you are alone with a balloon, outside of a school setting?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I think I notice it much more at school. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same reaction to balloons if you were to see them in a different school, or is it something about your current school specifically that contributes to your reaction?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it would happen in another school too, if I saw balloons there. I don’t know if it’s only my current school, exactly. What brings me in is that at school, the second I spot balloons—even across a hallway—my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing.  \n\nSo I guess it’s more the school setting with balloons than just that one building, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding going to places or events where you think balloons might be present?\n\nPatient: A little bit, yeah… I mean, I still try to do what I’m supposed to do, but lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circles if there are balloons involved. And if there are festival tables or decorations with balloons nearby, I kind of move away from that area.\n\nIf I absolutely have to stay, I just keep some distance and focus on getting through it without looking at them too much. I hope that makes sense—I’m not trying to avoid my responsibilities, exactly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you have to avoid places or events because of balloons?\n\nPatient: It’s… uncomfortable, but I try not to make a huge thing of it. I’d say it’s enough that I change what I’m doing, like asking my aides to handle birthday circles if there are balloons, or I’ll move away from festival tables where they’re set up.\n\nIf I have to stay, I can usually manage by keeping some distance and just not looking at them much. But I do feel embarrassed about it, and kind of tense the whole time. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a social or work obligation because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes… a few times, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. At work, it’s started to get in the way a little. I’ve had to ask my aides to take over normal classroom celebrations when balloons were there, and I missed helping with a festival art table that I had actually planned because there were balloons nearby.\n\nMy supervisor noticed that one, which was embarrassing. I still try to do my job, but… yes, I guess I have avoided some work things because of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine that you have completely stopped doing because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… I don’t think I’d put it that strongly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of balloons is interfering with your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… mostly at work. I don’t think my friends have really been affected much, but with colleagues it’s been awkward because I’ve had to hand off normal classroom celebrations to my aides when balloons are involved.\n\nAnd there was that festival art table I was supposed to help with, the one I had planned, and I didn’t end up doing it because there were balloons nearby. My supervisor noticed, which made me feel pretty embarrassed. I hope I’m explaining it okay — it’s not that I’m trying to avoid people, exactly, it just ends up looking that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of balloons is affecting your overall job performance?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t think I’m doing a terrible job or anything, but it has started to affect work in some situations.\n\nLike, when there are balloons for classroom celebrations, I’ve had to hand that off to my aides instead of handling it myself. I mean, I’ll avoid them if I notice them, or I might choose to stand somewhere else at an event, but I haven’t really stopped doing important things outside of work because of it.\n\nI guess it’s more inconvenient than life-limiting outside the classroom, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're avoiding a significant part of your life because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say a significant part of my life, exactly… at least I don’t think so. It’s not like balloons are on my mind all day.\n\nBut if I know there’s going to be a school event or a birthday thing, I might wonder ahead of time if there will be balloons there. I try not to dwell on it, though. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not usually fully turning things down, no… I don’t want to make it seem bigger than it is.

But lately I have found myself kind of working around it. Like at school, if there’s a birthday circle and I know there are balloons, I might ask one of my aides to handle that part. Or if I’m at a festival or something and there’s a table with balloons nearby, I’ll just move away from that area.

If I really have to stay, I try to keep some distance and just focus on getting through it without looking at them too much. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess what brings me in is something that feels a little silly to say out loud, but at school, if I see balloons—even just across the hallway—my chest tightens right away and I kind of freeze for a moment. It’s like I can’t think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing for a few seconds. I know that probably sounds small, but it’s been enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there other things besides balloons that cause this kind of reaction in you?\n\nPatient: Not really other things, no… it’s mostly balloons specifically. I mean, I know they’re just decorations, especially in a classroom, and I feel a bit embarrassed saying this, but when one is stretched really tight or bobbing near the children, I immediately feel like it could pop right in my face. And I get scared in a way that feels… bigger than it should, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Has anything unpleasant or traumatic ever happened to you involving balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I wouldn’t call it traumatic exactly, maybe that sounds too dramatic, but about seven months ago a balloon burst really close to my face during a children’s art celebration. Since then, this has kind of been happening. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction happen if you see a balloon in a different context, like at home or in a picture?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mainly at school that I notice it, especially if I spot balloons even from across the hallway. It’s almost immediate—my chest tightens and I sort of freeze for a moment before I can get back to what I’m supposed to be doing. I know that probably sounds like a lot for just seeing a balloon, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Does the reaction to balloons happen when you are alone with a balloon, outside of a school setting?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I think I notice it much more at school. Like, the second I spot balloons there, even from down the hallway, my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing. \n\nIf I were alone with one somewhere else… I imagine I’d still feel uneasy, but I guess the school setting is where it really shows up for me. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same reaction to balloons if you were to see them in a different school, or is it something about your current school specifically that contributes to your reaction?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it would happen in another school too, if I saw balloons there. I don’t know if it’s only my current school, exactly. What brings me in is that at school, the second I spot balloons—even across a hallway—my chest tightens and I kind of freeze for a moment before I can think clearly about what I’m supposed to be doing.  \n\nSo I guess it’s more the school setting with balloons than just that one building, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding going to places or events where you think balloons might be present?\n\nPatient: A little bit, yeah… I mean, I still try to do what I’m supposed to do, but lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circles if there are balloons involved. And if there are festival tables or decorations with balloons nearby, I kind of move away from that area.\n\nIf I absolutely have to stay, I just keep some distance and focus on getting through it without looking at them too much. I hope that makes sense—I’m not trying to avoid my responsibilities, exactly.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when you have to avoid places or events because of balloons?\n\nPatient: It’s… uncomfortable, but I try not to make a huge thing of it. I’d say it’s enough that I change what I’m doing, like asking my aides to handle birthday circles if there are balloons, or I’ll move away from festival tables where they’re set up.\n\nIf I have to stay, I can usually manage by keeping some distance and just not looking at them much. But I do feel embarrassed about it, and kind of tense the whole time. I’m not sure if that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided a social or work obligation because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Yes… a few times, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. At work, it’s started to get in the way a little. I’ve had to ask my aides to take over normal classroom celebrations when balloons were there, and I missed helping with a festival art table that I had actually planned because there were balloons nearby.\n\nMy supervisor noticed that one, which was embarrassing. I still try to do my job, but… yes, I guess I have avoided some work things because of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your daily routine that you have completely stopped doing because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped… I don’t think I’d put it that strongly. I’m still doing my normal day-to-day things, mostly.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been adjusting around it—like asking my aides to handle birthday circles if there are balloons, or moving away from festival tables when balloons are nearby. And if I really have to stay, I keep some distance and just try to get through it without looking at them too much.\n\nSo… not completely stopped, I guess, but I am changing how I handle certain parts of the day. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of balloons is interfering with your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… mostly at work. I don’t think my friends have really been affected much, but with colleagues it’s been awkward because I’ve had to hand off normal classroom celebrations to my aides when balloons are involved.\n\nAnd there was that festival art table I was supposed to help with, the one I had planned, and I didn’t end up doing it because there were balloons nearby. My supervisor noticed, which made me feel pretty embarrassed. I hope I’m explaining it okay — it’s not that I’m trying to avoid people, exactly, it just ends up looking that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of balloons is affecting your overall job performance?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t think I’m doing a terrible job or anything, but it has started to affect work in some situations.\n\nLike, when there are balloons for classroom celebrations, I’ve had to hand that off to my aides instead of handling it myself. And I missed helping with a festival art table that I had planned because there were balloons nearby, and my supervisor noticed that.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it is affecting my job performance somewhat, even if I’m trying to manage it and not let it show too much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work, where you feel like your fear of balloons is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Um… besides work, I’m not sure it’s causing big problems, honestly. I mean, I’ll avoid them if I notice them, or I might choose to stand somewhere else at an event, but I haven’t really stopped doing important things outside of work because of it.\n\nI guess it’s more inconvenient than life-limiting outside the classroom, if that makes sense. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're avoiding a significant part of your life because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say a significant part of my life, exactly… at least I don’t think so. But lately I have been avoiding certain parts of things.\n\nLike at school, if there are balloons for birthday circles, I’ll ask my aides to handle that part. And at events, if there’s a table or area with balloons nearby, I’ll kind of move away or not stay there. If I absolutely have to be near them, I try to keep some distance and just focus on getting through it without looking at them too much.\n\nI know that probably counts as avoiding, but I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of balloons is something that you think about or worry about even when you're not actually seeing balloons?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I worry about it constantly or anything like that. It’s not like balloons are on my mind all day.\n\nBut if I know there’s going to be a school event or a birthday thing, I might wonder ahead of time if there will be balloons there. I try not to dwell on it, though. I’m not sure if that counts as worrying, exactly… but it does come up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious before going to a place where you think balloons might be present?\n\nPatient: Yes… a little, I guess. If I’m going somewhere and I think there might be balloons, I do get kind of tense beforehand.\n\nIt’s not that I think something terrible is definitely going to happen, but when I picture one of those tight balloons, or one bobbing around near kids, I start thinking it could pop right near me. I know it’s probably just decoration and not a big deal, but in the moment it feels bigger than it should. Like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it.\n\nI don’t know if that makes sense, or if I’m explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience excessive anxiety or fear in response to other specific objects or situations, aside from balloons?\n\nPatient: No, not really… at least nothing I can think of in the same way.\n\nI mean, there are normal things I don’t love, like anyone, but I don’t think I have that kind of reaction to other objects or situations. It’s mostly just the balloons. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever turned down a social invitation because of the possibility that balloons might be present?\n\nPatient: Not usually fully turning things down, no… I don’t want to make it seem bigger than it is.\n\nBut lately I have found myself kind of working around it. I mean… I wouldn’t want to say it’s ruining my life or anything like that. Overall I still do what I need to do.

But yes, I guess it has started to affect things more than I’d like to admit, especially at work. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same reaction to balloons if you were to see them in a different school, or is it something about your current school specifically that contributes to your reaction?\n\nPatient: I think… probably it would happen in another school too, if I saw balloons there. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your fear of balloons is interfering with your relationships with colleagues or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… mostly at work. I don’t think my friends have really been affected much, but with colleagues it’s been awkward because I’ve had to hand off normal classroom celebrations to my aides when balloons are involved.\n\nAnd there was that festival art table I was supposed to help with, the one I had planned, and I didn’t end up doing it because there were balloons nearby. My supervisor noticed, which made me feel pretty embarrassed. I hope I’m explaining it okay — it’s not that I’m trying to avoid people, exactly, it just ends up looking that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of balloons is affecting your overall job performance?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t think I’m doing a terrible job or anything, but it has started to affect work in some situations.\n\nLike, when there are balloons for classroom celebrations, I’ve had to hand that off to my aides instead of handling it myself. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're avoiding a significant part of your life because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say a significant part of my life, exactly… at least I don’t think so. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of balloons is significantly interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t want to say it’s ruining my life or anything like that. Overall I still do what I need to do.\n\nBut yes, I guess it has started to affect things more than I’d like to admit, especially at work. I’ve had to hand off normal classroom celebrations to my aides when balloons are involved, and there was a festival art table I’d planned to help with that I ended up missing because balloons were nearby. My supervisor noticed, which was… embarrassing.\n\nSo maybe not my whole quality of life, but it is getting in the way in ways I don’t feel great about. Does that make sense?"
